### GLOSSARY OF TERMS

Agricultural Lands - Land devoted principally to the planting of trees, raising of crops, livestock and poultry, dairying, salt making, inland fishing and similar aquacultural activities, and other agricultural activities.

Barangay - The smallest political unit in the country. Buffer Zones - Identified Areas outside the boundaries of and immediately adjacent to designated protected areas pursuant to Section 8 of RA 7586. Cadastral Map - A public record of land ownership. Carbon Footprint - Measure of the impact of human activities on the environment in terms of greenhouse gases produced, measured in units of carbon dioxide

Choke Point - Areas of traffic congestion resulting from the convergence of two or more roads and other factors such as road narrowing, illegal loading and unloading of vehicles, and traffic flow enforcement. Choke points are characterized by the built-up of vehicle volume in a given area of their slow movement.

Climate Change - Refers to a change in climate that can be identified by changes in the mean and/or variability of its properties and that persists for an extended period typically decades or longer, whether due to natural variability or as a result of human activity

Comprehensive Land Use Plan - Refers to a document embodying specific proposals for guiding, regulating growth and development of a city or municipality. It is comprehensive because it considers all sectors significant in the development process such as demography, socio-economic, infrastructure and utilities, land use and local administration, within the territorial jurisdiction.

Commercial Zone - Areas designated principally for trade, services and business purposes. Crude Birth Rate - The annual number of live births per 1,000 population. Crude Death Rate - The annual number of deaths per 1,000 population. Demography - The study of human population, including its size, composition, distribution, density, growth, and other demographic and socioeconomic characteristics. Dependency Ratio - The ratio of the population below 15 and over 64 years old to the population belonging to the age bracket 15-64 years old.

Disaster Risk Reduction - Refers to the concept and practice of reducing disaster risks through systematic efforts to in analyzing and managing the causal factors of disasters, including through reduced exposure to hazards, lessened vulnerability of people and property, wise management of land and environment, and improved preparedness for adverse events.

Drainage - Process of removing ground water or surface water by artificial or natural means. Easement - Open space imposed on any land use/activities sited along waterways, roadright-of- way, cemeteries/memorial parks and utilities.

- xi


Environmental Planner - A person engaged in the practice of environmental planning and duly registered with the Board of Environmental Planning of the Professional Regulation Commission in the manner provided by law.

Environmental Planning - Activities concerned with the management and development of land, as well as the preservation, conservation and rehabilitation of the human environment.

Flood Prone Areas - Low lying areas usually adjacent to large or active water bodies and therefore experience regular or seasonal inundation as a result of changes in the mean water level of these bodies.

Geologic Hazards - Volcanoes, fault lines, land slips, unstable/landslide areas which pose risks to lives, crops, property, and infrastructures.

Goal Achievement Matrix - A plan evaluation method which is deemed superior to the cost benefit analysis and the planning balance sheet. It is highly participatory and allows various sectors of the community to express their bias through the weighing of the objectives according to their relative importance to their sectors.

Household - A social unit consisting of a person or a group of persons who occupy and sleep in the same dwelling unit and have common arrangement for the preparation and consumption of food.

Housing Backlog - Phenomenon where lodging and accommodations become insufficient for the population. Housing backlogs occur as a result of either the tremendous increase in population in which homes and lodging facilities become too scarce to accommodate them or due to inflated prices imposed on homes, which low-income earners are unable to afford.

Inclusionary Zoning - The process of delineating where affordable housing and market-rate housing are included. In practice, inclusionary zoning involves creating policies to place deed restrictions on 10% to 30% of new houses or apartments in order to market the costs of housing more affordable to lower-income households. This has been used to avoid the creation and retention of inner city ghettos or residential enclaves of low-income earners.

Index Crimes - Crimes that are significant and which occur with sufficient regularity to be meaningful. This includes murder, physical injury, robbery, theft, and rape.

Internal Revenue Allotment (IRA) - Share of local government units in the national internal revenue taxes based on the collection of the third fiscal year preceding the current fiscal year.

Literacy Rate - The percentage of the population who have completed at least a year in elementary education to the population seven years old and older.

Local Development Council - A body established in each local government unit to assist the corresponding Sanggunian in setting the direction of economic and social development, and in coordinating development efforts within its territorial jurisdiction.

Mixed-Use Development - Development characterized by two or more significant revenue producing uses such as retail, office, residential, hotel/motel, entertainment/cultural/ recreation that in well-planned projects are mutually supporting and compatible.

Morbidity - The measure of the prevalence of a disease afflicting a given population

- xii


Mortality - A measure of the number of deaths, in general or due to specific causes, in a given population per unit time

Non-Index Crimes - Criminal offenses consisting of negligent manslaughter, non-aggravated assault, forgery, counterfeit, fraud, embezzlement, stolen property, vandalism, weapons, prostitution, sex offenses, narcotics laws, gambling, offenses against family and children, drinking under the influence of alcohol, and disorderly conduct.

Policy - Set of related principles and rules of action/conditions that would govern the pursuit of goals and objectives.

Population Density - Number of persons per unit of land area (square kilometers or square miles).

Sex Ratio - The number of males per 100 females in a population.

Transect - A cross-section of the environment showing a range of different habitats. The ruralurban transect of the human environment used in the Smart Code template is divided into six transect zones. These zones describe the physical form and character of a place, according to the density and intensity of its land use and urbanism.

Transportation Map - A map that indicates data on circulation system within the planning areas. These include bridges, roads, airports, harbors, water bodies. The location type and make of transportation infrastructure are also indicated.

Urbanization - Growth in the proportion of a population living in urban areas. It is said that urbanization takes place when there is development. It has also been established that economic opportunities in an area stimulate in-migration, thus resulting in the increasing density of the population. With population increase, comes further development to meet the needs of the growing population, in which becomes urbanization.

Vertical Urbanism - Intensification and densification of areas thru vertical development to cater to the community's residential, commercial, institutional, and mixed-use needs. It also seeks to provide more open spaces and to lessen the building's footprint.

Water Quality - The characteristics of water which define its use in terms of physical, chemical, and biological contents. Hence, the quality of water for domestic use is different from industrial use.

Zoning Ordinance - A local legal measure which embodies regulations affecting land use. The preparation of a zoning ordinance is based on the Comprehensive Land Use Plan and is enacted by the Local Sanggunian through a resolution. Such ordinance shall be presented, in its full context, as a separate document

- xiii

HISTORICAL BACKGROUND Pre-Hispanic to Spanish Colonization

The City was known as San Pedro Tunasan, which was derived from the plant named Tunas, a type of water lily that is abundant in the area. Tunasan was originally part of the town of Tabuko, known as Cabuyao in the present along with the cities of Biñan and Sta. Rosa. As the Spaniards occupied the central area of the country and got hold of the City of Manila in 1574, nearby towns in Rizal and those bordering the Laguna de Bay were also occupied until they have reached the town of Tabuko.

Conquistador Esteban Rodriguez de Figueroa entrusted the management of the land to the Jesuit group, Colegio de San Jose, spreading as well Roman Catholicism in the area. The town then was called the Hacienda de San Pedro de Tunasan. By January 1725, Tunasan has been officially separated from Tabuko and became a town of its own along with the first nayons or barangays, Nayon ng San Roque, Poblacion, Cuyab, Landayan and San Antonio. The ownership of Tunasan was transferred eventually to the rector of the University of Sto. Tomas due to issues in power and authority by the Jesuits. In the revolution against the Spaniards, San Pedro became the source of the needed logistics for the battle, and refuge of the country’s military forces, while gradually participating in the rebellion.

###### American Occupation

In 1903, due to insufficient financial capacity of the San Pedro, the town was merged with Biñan. By 1907, San Pedro Tunasan was then separated from Biñan and had its own municipal president. In 1914, the name of the town was changed into what is now known as San Pedro.

With the changes in the administration, unjust management of the town were observed which resulted to Commonwealth Act, indicating the buying of all the lands and distribution of such to the poor people. 218 hectares of land were bought and were resold to the bonafide residents of San Pedro through the Rural Progress Administration.

###### Japanese Occupation, Post-Second World War to Present

The US Army Forces in the Far East (USAFFE) utilized the town’s roads for their retreat to the Province of Bataan. The Filipinos’ resistance caused several San Pedrenses to join the war through the guerilla movement until the end of the World War II in 1945. Civil government was then established in the whole country.

In 1954, with the ongoing struggles on the ownership of the lands, President Ramon Magsaysay bought the remaining land area of San Pedro and entrusted the redistribution to the National Resettlement and Rural Administration (NARRA).

Real estate developments, as well as commercial and industrial establishments have been continuously emerging since 1970’s, dubbing the city as a “dormitory town” due to its proximity to the Metropolitan Manila.

In 2013, San Pedro became a city under the Republic Act No. 10420.

## SITUATIONAL ANALYSIS

![image 86](912157126-San-Pedro-Comprehensive-Development-Plan_images/imageFile86.png)

### Introduction

![image 87](912157126-San-Pedro-Comprehensive-Development-Plan_images/imageFile87.png)

#### Plan Objectives, Context and Coverage

###### 1.1.1.1 Plan Objectives

The San Pedro Comprehensive Development Plan is the primary technical guide for the development of the City of San Pedro.

Its specific objectives are the following:

- 1. Define the overall vision, thrusts and strategies for the City.
- 2. Provide the analytical basis for understanding the existing conditions of the various sectors specifically the key issues and concerns, its impacts and effects.
- 3. Come up with interventions such as legislations, programs, projects and activities to attain the goals, objectives and strategies.


###### 1.1.1.2 Plan Context

The City Comprehensive Development Plan is a vital link to the network of plans at the national, regional and provincial levels. Investment plans and programs at the City level have vertical connections to the Provincial Development and Physical Framework Plan in terms of policies, strategies and priorities.

At the City level, the programs, projects and activities (PPAs) derived from the CDP for the 3year Local Development Investment Plan (LDIP) and the Annual Investment Plan (AIP), serves as the basis for the budgetary allocation of the PPAs.

###### 1.1.1.3 Plan Coverage

- a. Historical Coverage The San Pedro Comprehensive Development Plan (CDP) is a six - year development plan covering the planning period 2016 to 2021 and serves as a continuing planning reference and guide to the development direction of the City.
- b. Geographical Coverage The political boundaries of the City define the primary level of the geographical analysis. Other cities and towns in the province as well as the province itself and the country as a whole are used for benchmarking. The barangays within the City serve as the major level of analytical disaggregation for selected population analysis.
- c. Sector Coverage The planning environment of the CDP encompasses all major sectors relevant to the development of the City. The core elements considered are: Physical Environment, Population


and Settlement, Local Economy, Social Development, Climate Change and Disaster Risk Mitigation, Infrastructure, Urban Renewal and Institutional Development.

The interrelatedness and interdependence of these core elements are part of the analysis specifically in the formulation of sector goals and objectives and the identification of strategies, targets, plans, programs and projects for each sector.

![image 88](912157126-San-Pedro-Comprehensive-Development-Plan_images/imageFile88.png)

- 1. Vision, Thrusts and Strategies

- • Population and Settlement
- • Local Economy
- • Social Development – Health, Education, Social Welfare, Housing and Resettlement, Peace and Order
- • Climate Change and Disaster Risk Mitigation

- • Urban Renewal

- 3. Regional and Provincial Development Perspectives

- • Laguna de Bay Basin Master Plan: 2016 and Beyond
- • Calabarzon Regional Development Plan, 2010-2016
- • Laguna Provincial Development and Physical Framework Plan, 2010- 2020


- 4. Development Plans

- • Social Development Plan
- • Economic Development Plan
- • Infrastructure and Land Use Development Plan
- • Environmental Management, Climate Change Adaptation and Disaster Risk Mitigation Plan
- • Urban Renewal Sector Development Plan
- • Institutional Sector Development Plan


- 5. Contents of Each Sector Development Plan


- • Goal
- • Objectives
- • Strategies
- • Targets
- • Sector Analysis Matrix
- • SWOT Analysis
- • Legislative Requirements
- • Project Ideas

#### Legal Basis, Organization and Management

###### 1.1.3.1 Legal Bases: National, Regional and Provincial Policies on Planning Applicable tothe City/Municipality

- a. Executive Order No. 72 providing for the preparation and implementation of the Comprehensive Land Use Plans (CLUPs) of the LGUs.
- b. Republic Act No. 7160 providing for the Local Government Code specifically Section 20 on the reclassification of agricultural lands to non-agricultural

uses. Section 114 providing for the approval of the Local Development Plans by the Local Development Council and the Sangguniang Bayan/Panlungsod.

- c. Memorandum Circular No. 54 prescribing guidelines governing Section 20 of RA 7160 authorizing cities and municipalities to reclassify agricultural lands into non-agricultural uses.
- d. Administrative Order No. 20 specifying lands which are non-negotiable for land conversion.
- e. Executive Order No. 124 establishing priorities and procedures in the evaluation of development areas proposed for land conversion in regional, agro-industrial centers, tourism development areas and sites for socialized housing.
- f. Republic Act No. 7279 known as the Urban Development and Housing Act mandating LGUs to identify sites for socialized housing and their integration into CLUPs and Zoning Ordinance of their cities and municipalities.
- g. Executive Order No. 71 providing for the devolution of powers of the HLURB to approve subdivision plans to cities and municipalities pursuant to the Local Government Code of 1991.
- h. Memorandum No. 33 directing the integration and operationalization of the goals and objectives of sustainable development contained in the Philippine Agenda 21 and Philippines Strategy for Sustainable Development into the updated Philippine Medium Term Development Plan into the plans of the LGUs.
- i. Executive Order 15 providing for the creation of the Philippine Council for Sustainable Development.
- j. Republic Act 7586 on the National Integrated Protected Areas (NIPAS) mandating the protection of strict nature reserve, national park, natural monument, wildlife sanctuary, protected landscapes and seascapes, resource reserve, natural biotic areas, and other categories established by law, conventions or international agreements of which the Philippines is a signatory.
- k. Republic Act 8749 called the Philippine Clear Air Act of 1999 providing for a comprehensive air pollution control policy and for other purposes.
- l. Republic Act 9003 called Ecological Solid Waste Management Act of 2000 adopting systematic, comprehensive and ecological solid waste management program that shall ensure the protection of public health and environment.
- m. Republic Act 9275 called Clean Water Act of 2004 protecting the country’s water bodies from pollution from land based sources (industries, commercial establishments, agriculture, community and households).
- n. Republic Act 8435 called the Agriculture and Fisheries Modernization Act of 1997 prescribing urgent measures to modernize the agriculture and fisheries sectors for the challenges of globalization.


- o. Republic Act 9593 called the Tourism Act of 2009 providing for tourism governance, tourism officer, tourism assistance and tourism accreditation system.
- p. Republic Act 9729 called the Climate Change Act of 2009 creating the Climate Change Commission tasked to formulate the National Climate Change Action Plan in consultation with government agencies.
- q. Administrative Order No. 1, 1994 creating the Provincial Land Use Committee (PLUC) to assist the Provincial Planning and Development Office (PPDO) in the formulation of the Provincial Physical Framework Plan (PPFP).
- r. Administrative Order No. 2 of 1994 creating the Provincial Development Council (PDC) to formulate a Provincial Development Plan (PDP) to uplift the standard of living of the people of Laguna.
- s. Republic Act 10121 called Philippine Disaster Risk and Reduction and Management Act of 2020 providing for the development of policies, plans and the implementation of action and measures pertaining to all aspects of disaster risk reduction and management

### The Physical Environment

![image 91](912157126-San-Pedro-Comprehensive-Development-Plan_images/imageFile91.png)

#### Key Issues and Concerns

- 1. Fluvial and lakeshore flooding due to heavy rains and storms.
- 2. Earthquake fault system and soil erosion/landslides induced by heavy rains.
- 3. Solid and liquid waste pollution from informal settlers, households and commercial establishments like used oil and grease.
- 4. Addressing Climate Change Adaptation concerns.
- 5. Depleting ground water resources by commercial, industrial and residential deep wells.
- 6. Air pollution and foul odor from piggeries, paint sprays, vehicle smoke emissions.
- 7. Deforestation of the watershed areas in the upland due to uncontrolled human settlements.
- 8. Need to introduce catchment technology to capture and retain rainwater and runoff especially in the water starved areas.
- 9. Heavy siltation of bodies of water as a result of deforestation, soil erosion and illegal disposal of solid waste.
- 10. Danger of water-borne diseases in untreated water supply.


###### 1.2.1.1 Sector Description

The environmental sector is highly complex. The continuing industrialization and urbanization greatly affects and contributes in the rapid deterioration of the quality of our environment. Flooding, soil erosion, air pollution, water pollution and soil contamination are effects of our negligence in caring for our environment thus, global warming and climate change are now felt and different measures must be undertaken to address the issues affecting our environment. Therefore, the Comprehensive Development Plan is prepared to guide us in the future development of the City of San Pedro.

![image 92](912157126-San-Pedro-Comprehensive-Development-Plan_images/imageFile92.png)

Terrestrial Environment

###### 1.2.2.1 Geography and Location

The City of San Pedro is a third-class component city of the Province of Laguna. It is one of the thirty (30) LGUs and one (1) of the six (6) component cities in the Province. The city is located in the western part of Laguna Province, adjacent to the National Capital Region (NCR). It is bounded by the City of Muntinlupa in the north, the City of Biñan, Laguna and Municipality of Carmona, Cavite in the south, Laguna de Bay in the east and City of Dasmariñas, Cavite in the west, with the geographic coordinates of 121°00’ to 121°05’ latitudes and 14°20’ to 14°25’ longitudes. It is approximately twenty-nine (29) kilometers south of Manila via South Luzon Expressway and sixtyone (61) kilometers from the provincial capital of Laguna, the municipality of Sta. Cruz.

The total land area of the city is 2,405 hectares, comprising about 1.22 percent of the total land area of Laguna. About 1,680.17 has. (72%) of the city is built-up area while 655.52 has. (28%) is non-built-up area. San Pedro is comprised of 27 barangays which are distributed between lower and upper barangays based on the common geophysical characteristics. Brgy. San Antonio has the largest area with 855.62 has.

(36.63%), while Brgy. Narra has the least area with 5.61 has. (0.24%) of the total land area. Brgy. Poblacion with a land area of 31.16 has. (1.33%) is the center of the city, having trade and commerce as the main economic activities due to the presence of numerous business establishments and buildings.

![image 96](912157126-San-Pedro-Comprehensive-Development-Plan_images/imageFile96.png)

#### Topography and Slopes

The elevation of the city ranges from 0-106 meters above sea level (masl). The eastern portion which are bounded by the Laguna Lake are the areas with low elevation, while the central and western portion have gradually increasing elevations, peaking at 106 masl at Brgy. Langgam and some areas in Brgy. San Antonio.

Majority of the city’s topography is flat, where 65 percent of the total land area has 0-8 percent slope, while 35 percent of its land area has a slope greater than 8 percent.

San Pedro consists of volcanic tuff materials and alluvial deposits. Volcanic Tuff materials are located in the elevated western portions of the city, comprising of volcanic plain or piedmont deposit and chief basaltic pyroclastic debris which has considerable water bearing capacities. Alluvium deposits consists of heterogeneous sand and silt clays along valley floor flood plains and coastal plains and are located in the eastern portion of the city. These however are prone to liquefaction.

Soil classes that are present in San Pedro are Carmona series and Guadalupe series. Carmona series covers majority of the land area (68%) which are suitable for both agricultural or urban uses. The remaining land area, generally in lowland areas are found with Guadalupe soil series, which are best suited to rice, corn, root crops and cogon grass.

San Pedro has a slope gradient that ranges from level to very steep hills. Majority of land area falls under the level category to very gently sloping category of 0-3 mostly in the eastern side. (See table 3).

SOURCE: NAMRIA Topographic Map

By virtue of LLDA Resolution No. 248 series of 2005, the lakeshore of Laguna Lake has significant ecological importance and thereby it imposes restrictions on all developments situated in the areas. These areas are identified as lands adjacent to Laguna Lake with elevation below 12.5 meters that is exposed or submerged by the annual raising and lowering of the lake waters. For this reason, at least 29 percent of the eastern side of San Pedro falls under this restriction.

###### 1.2.3.1 Geophysical Attributes

Geology. San Pedro has varying origin of rocks such as volcanic tuff materials and alluvial deposits. Alluvium or fluviatile alluvium deposits can be found on the eastern side consisting of heterogeneous sand and silt clays along valley floor flood plains and coastal plains.

Pyroclastic can be found on the western side consisting of volcanic plain or volcanic piedmont deposit, chief basaltic pyroclastic (ashes, cinder, bomb, tuff and other volcano debris prevalent at the foot of volcanoes. Alluvium is considered prone to liquefaction while pyroclastics are considered good for its water bearing capacities.

Soil Types. San Pedro has two soil type series: Carmona and Guadalupe series. Carmona series can be further sub-grouped into Carmona sandy clay loam and Carmona clay loam. Guadalupe series can be sub-grouped into Guadalupe clay and Guadalupe clay adobe.

Majority of land area of about 1,537 hectares or 68 percent belongs to the Carmona series marginally suitable for agricultural purposes but moderately to highly suitable for urban uses.

About 339 hectares has Carmona sandy clay loam suitable for lowland rice, corn, vegetables, sugarcane and bananas. Carmona clay loam is suitable for mangoes, ipil-ipil, sugar cane upland rice, corn, bananas, sweet potatoes and pineapples.

The Guadalupe soil series are found in the lowland areas suitable to rice, corn, root crops and cogon grass. The town center and boundary between Muntinlupa and Dasmarinas are made of Guadalupe series. Of the 723 hectares of Guadalupe series, 565 hectares are Guadalupe clay and 158 hectares are Guadalupe clay adobe.

###### 1.2.3.2 Geologic Hazards

Earthquake and Seismicity. The geographical and geological setting of San Pedro makes it prone to seismic and hydrologic hazards due to the Valley Fault System composed of the nonactive East Valley Fault and the active West Valley Fault. The West Valley Fault passes through San Pedro that makes it highly susceptible to ground shaking, liquefaction and surface faulting or rupturing.

###### Figure 1. Valley Fault System Crossing Barangays

![image 97](912157126-San-Pedro-Comprehensive-Development-Plan_images/imageFile97.png)

SOURCE: Manila Geosciences Bureau

Floods. Two types of flooding in San Pedro – fluvial or river and Laguna lakeshore flooding. Fluvial flooding occurs when excessive rainfall causes capacity of heavily silted rivers to overflow called flash floods. Lakeshore flooding is caused by the rising of the lake water level (12 to 14 meters elevation) inundating the coastal communities.

Siltation is caused by soil erosions going to the river with no slope protection and solid wastes disposal especially by informal sector families positioned along the bodies of water.

The drainage system needs improvement as many conduits of water such as irrigation canals and creeks are no longer functional especially in residential and commercial areas resulting in storm water overflow in the paved roads. Existing drainages, covered and lineal canals are in bad conditions requiring reconstruction to accommodate the actual volume of flood waters during heavy rains.

###### Map 4. Flood Hazard Map, San Pedro, Laguna

![image 98](912157126-San-Pedro-Comprehensive-Development-Plan_images/imageFile98.png)

SOURCE: Manila Geosciences Bureau

![image 99](912157126-San-Pedro-Comprehensive-Development-Plan_images/imageFile99.png)

#### Water Resources

River. San Pedro River is sourced from the non-existent watershed areas of the upland areas near Cavite Province due to human settlements, commercial and industrial developments. The quality of water has decreased with high level of pollution from residential areas not suitable for aquaculture, fisheries, recreation and water extraction for industrial uses. The river acts as a natural boundary from Muntinlupa before it drains into Laguna Lake. The informal settlers occupy the banks of San Vicente River with 229 families, San Antonio River with 273 families and Magsaysay River with 110 families.

###### Map 5. River System/Surface Water Map, San Pedro, Laguna

![image 100](912157126-San-Pedro-Comprehensive-Development-Plan_images/imageFile100.png)

SOURCE: Manila Geosciences Bureau

Laguna Lake. Ebbing the eastern shores of San Pedro is the largest lake in the Philippines, Laguna de Bay with a total surface area of more than 90,000 hectares. It has an average depth of 3 to 4 meters due to heavy siltation. There are more than 20 tributaries contributing to lake waters and among these are the San Pedro River and Tunasan River on the western side of the Lake.

###### Figure 2. Laguna de Bay Basin

![image 101](912157126-San-Pedro-Comprehensive-Development-Plan_images/imageFile101.png)

Source: National Mapping Resource Information Authority (NAMRIA)

Surface Water Quality. The DENR-LLDA conducts regular water quality monitoring through several stations in the upland and lowland areas of the San Pedro River and Tunasan River. The said rivers have the highest levels of contamination from solid and liquid wastes from domestic households, commercial establishments, piggeries, and industries. The effects are foul odor, fish kill, plants diseases and crop failures.

Ground Water Quality. The quality of groundwater is highly questionable as a result of solid and liquid wastes disposal methods. It is highly possible that leachate infiltrates into groundwater sources or intrudes via channels and fissures. Due to unregulated extraction, depletion of artesian water may lead to ground subsidence, sinkhole, liquefaction, foul odor and the movement activation of the earthquake fault line in certain areas.

![image 102](912157126-San-Pedro-Comprehensive-Development-Plan_images/imageFile102.png)

#### Climate Change Adaptation

Determinants and indicators of climate change with cause and effects are: rising temperature, high humidity, extreme rainfall intensity, severe storms and typhoons, rising level of the Laguna Lake waters, fish kill, drought, greenhouse gases, loss of marine organisms, food scarcity, ground water depletion and the emergence of heat and tropical related diseases, such as stroke, the spread of virus and bacteria, dengue and respiratory diseases, among others.

The climate in San Pedro falls under Type I of the Modified Coronas Climate Classification, characterized by two pronounced seasons, specifically dry from November to April and wet during the rest of the year. Using the climatological normals obtained from the nearest Automatic Weather Station (AWS) in the region which is the Ambulong, Batangas AWS, the average annual mean temperature in the city for period 1991-2020 is 27.7°C. The month of May is the warmest time of the year with an average of 29.3°C, while the month of January is the coolest with an average temperature of 26.3°C. The average temperatures differ during the year by 3°C.

The average rainfall in San Pedro is 151.34mm. Rainfall is the lowest in March with an average of 21.0 mm while the most precipitation falls in July with an average of 338.3mm. The difference between the driest and wettest month is 317.3°C.

Southwest wind prevails over San Pedro during the months of January, October, November and December. Northeast wind is predominant in July, August and September while Northwest wind prevails in February, March and April.

Typhoon Occurrence. The mean percentage frequency of typhoon passage within the city, as in most Laguna towns, ranges from 11%-30%. This means that out of 19 typhoons which enter the Philippine Area of Responsibility (PAR), about 2-6 typhoons are expected to pass over the City of San Pedro.

#### Air Quality

Air quality in terms of particulates may be well within the allowable limits which can be seen by data from the DENR air pollution index. Smog prevalence in the major urban areas of Metro Manila is not obtained in the blue skies of San Pedro.

However, there are concerns related to foul odor from piggeries, diesel vehicle emissions and paint sprays, among others. Noise from vehicles and from other sources are easily transmitted by air/wind direction and circulation.

![image 104](912157126-San-Pedro-Comprehensive-Development-Plan_images/imageFile104.png)

#### Waste Management

Liquid Wastes. San Pedro lacks a city wide waste water and septage treatment facilities except septic tanks discharging wastes directly into the surface waters like rivers, creeks, drainage canals, contributing to the unfavorable Class C status of Laguna Lake. Fish kills, water pollution and foul odor are the end results. Used oil and grease are not properly disposed by industrial and commercial establishments and households.

In line with the implementation of the Ecological Solid Waste Management of 2000 mandating the LGU to practice solid waste segregation, only 65% of households and at least 50% of households from all barangays had been practicing waste segregation,

Solid Wastes. About 165 tons of solid waste are generated on a daily basis based on the computation using the projected population resulting in water, air and soil contamination. Most solid wastes especially non-hazardous domestic wastes are collected daily by the garbage contractor (Pilotage Trading) and directly disposed in the sanitary landfill area at Barangay San Antonio with a capacity of 200 metric tons daily.

There is an operational material recovery facility providing income to waste pickers. A methane gas extraction sourced from the landfill area is being operated by Bacavalley Energy producing

- 4 megawatt power of electricity fed to the Transcon Grid.


A centralized material recovery facility is not yet operational in the City although households practice on a small scale segregation. Biodegradable wastes are used for composting and recycled materials sold in the junkshops.

![image 105](912157126-San-Pedro-Comprehensive-Development-Plan_images/imageFile105.png)

#### Land Suitability

In general, assessment of land can be categorized into Environmentally Critical Areas (ECAs) and Non Environmentally Critical Areas (Non-ECAs). The ECAs are concentrated in flood prone and soil erosion areas in the uplands and earthquake fault line areas.

Land development must be pursued consistent with the land’s restricted physical features, social and economic best use and hazardous conditions.

![image 106](912157126-San-Pedro-Comprehensive-Development-Plan_images/imageFile106.png)

#### Hazards

The geographical and geological setting of San Pedro makes it prone to seismic and hydrologic hazards. It is important that these hazards are identified and made known to the public in order to prevent loss of life and minimize destruction of property.

Having the city bounded by the Laguna Lake on the east, such portion of the city is prone to flooding due to rise of water levels. Conversely, the elevation on the western portion of the city is midland to highland terrain peaking at 106 meters above sea level, specifically in Brgy. Langgam. Majority (75%) of the total land area has 0-3% slope or very gently sloping category, mostly on the eastern side of the city.

The city is also part of the Laguna Lake catchment basin, where the San Isidro River and Tunasan River are the major tributaries in the city. These also contribute to the occurrence of flooding within the city. The Valley Fault System, specifically the West Valley Fault passes through San Pedro, causing it to be prone to ground shaking, liquefaction and surface faulting/rupturing and hydrologic hazards such as flooding and erosion.

###### 1.2.9.1 Hazard Inventory Matrix

These are characterized hazards based on the following descriptors: Flood Susceptibility, Rain Induced Landslides, Storm surge/Laguna Lake level rise, Ground rupture, Ground shaking, Liquefaction, and earthquake induced Landslide.

Tsunami and Volcanic hazards are not applicable to our City for the City where volcanic hazards maybe affected starts from Calamba City, Laguna onwards southern areas. Our City was not fortunate to have sea and ocean

The most affected areas in flood susceptibility are the shoreline areas at Laguna Lake consisting of three Barangays, namely, Cuyab, San Roque and Landayan. However, Cuyab were the most hit by heavy rainfall and flooding while the citywide that causes ground rupture and shaking where susceptibility is high.

- 1.2.9.1.1 Geologic Hazards


Earthquake and Seismic Hazard. San Pedro is prone to earthquake hazards due to the Valley Fault System. It is one of the major faults in the region and is composed of the nonactive East Valley Fault and the active West Valley Fault. The West Valley fault passes through San Pedro, causing it to be prone to ground shaking, liquefaction, and surface faulting/rupturing.

Ground Rupture Hazard. Surface rupture occurs when movement on a fault breaks through to the surface. Rupture may occur suddenly during an earthquake or slowly in the form of fault creep. Fault rupture almost always follows pre-existing faults, which are zones of weakness.

Earthquakes are inevitable. Many people live and work near major faults that can produce strong ground shaking. Ground shaking is affected by several things: the size of the earthquake, the type of ground the earthquake waves travel through, and the distance away from the earthquake source. Every time there is a large earthquake, scientists learn more about how the energy released from the fault affects the ground and buildings around it. In the City of San Pedro, Laguna, the Barangays affected to Exposure Ground Rupture (faultline) hazard are San Antonio, GSIS, and United Bayanihan which are on high susceptibility level while Barangays Riverside and Calendola are in low susceptibility level.

Ground Shaking Hazard. Ground shaking is the primary cause of earthquake damage to man-made structures. When the ground shakes strongly, buildings can be damaged or destroyed and their occupants may be injured or killed. Seismologists have observed that some districts tend to repeatedly experience stronger seismic shaking than others.

With the occurrence of earthquake and faultlines, the City of San Pedro is 100% susceptible to ground shaking making the surroundings to be more vulnerable to damages of lives and properties.

Fault lines Hazard. The West Valley Fault transacted five (5) barangays of City of San Pedro as per the “Walk the Fault” activity by the City Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Council (CDRRMC) together with the Philippine Institute of Volcanology and Seismology (PHIVOLCS) of Department of Science and Technology (DOST) and Barangay Chairperson of affected barangays, held last June 22, 2015.

Five of its Barangays are affected namely:

Earthquake Induced Landslide. A gigantic rapid landslide claiming over 1,000 fatalities was triggered by rainfalls and a small nearby earthquake in the Leyte Island, Philippines in 2006. The disaster presented the necessity of a new modelling technology for disaster risk preparedness which simulates initiation and motion.

In San Pedro, Laguna, the PHIVOLCS, GMMA ATLAS has conducted the susceptibility level of earthquake induced landslide in the citywide which is on high level, particularly, on the upper Barangay or level area of the city with a magnitude of 6.8.

There is no historical occurrence on Tsunami, Volcanic eruption and other susceptible hazards wherein it is not significantly applicable in our city for we are quite far from these critical facilities.

- 1.2.9.1.2 Hydrologic Hazards


Hydrologic hazards include flooding and erosion. Two types of flooding occur in San Pedro, fluvial (river) and lakeshore flooding. Fluvial flooding occurs when excessive rainfall causes the capacity of rivers to overflow. Lakeshore flooding is caused by the rising of the Laguna de Bay water level. These are natural processes and cannot be avoided. Erosion is also a major hazard that causes topsoil to be carried away with flowing water. The following table shows areas of potential erosion in San Pedro.

Flooding. Flooding is perhaps one of the most destructive, frequent, and widespread hazards in the Philippines today. The effects of flooding can either be localized affecting only a small neighborhood or community or a very large area covering entire river basins.

There are several kinds of flooding. Some may be very slow which may take several days to dissipate while others are very quick which may take only several minutes and without any visible sign of rain. The latter is normally known as flash flood.

Flash floods normally have dangerous walls of raging waters carrying with it rocks, mud and other debris and can sweep away anything its path very easily. Dam breakage normally causes flash floods with a certain area. Overland flooding, on the other hand, occurs when defined rivers and streams overflow due to breakage of a levee.

Flood susceptibility level ranges from low to moderate level. Out of twenty Barangays, only five (5) barangays are moderately susceptible to flood hazard, which are, Barangays San Roque, Sto Nino, Poblacion, Landayan, and Cuyab while the rest were in a low susceptibility level.

Typhoon. Disaster Report of CSWD on Typhoon Ondoy last 2009 whereby number of affected persons had range at least to 7,625 persons with 1,525 families involve and counts of properties have been damaged. The most affected areas were the shoreline barangays and its susceptibility is high. Typhoon Ondoy became the wakeup call of the entire Filipino nation when it saw its devastating effects on Metro Manila, Laguna and Rizal. Unexpectedly, Ondoy became the worst nightmare for many in the metropolis and its neighboring provinces as they found themselves in their rooftops to save themselves from drowning. Many were crying for help because of the bitter cold and starvation. It was said that the amount of rainfall during Ondoy was more than the volume of rainfall that the Philippines would get in one year.

Millions were left homeless as they found their houses submerged in water. What was worse was the fact the flood was not subsiding. A lot of properties were destroyed especially cars, appliances, equipment, household furniture and more as they did not have time to secure them because of the unexpectedly rise in floodwater. Many were also left without food and clothing as they did not have time to pack.

Habagat. It is considered as the continuous heavy rainfall which is more devastating because it would cause flash flooding. Throughout the rest of the year, the Philippines experiences the west or southwest wind; southwest monsoon which is referred to as habagat. The habagat season (November –April) is characterized by hot and humid weather, frequent heavy rainfall and a prevailing wind from the west.

According to the 2012 Disaster Report, the “habagat” phenomenon affected mostly Barangay Cuyab whereby most areas were submerged because of the continued heavy rainfalls that lasted for days. About 7,587 persons and 1,821 families were affected.

###### Various Typhoons and other Disaster:

According to Disaster Report, the following typhoons had struck our city, namely, Typhoon Maring (2013), Typhoon Odette (2013), Typhoon Glenda (July 2014), tropical storm Mario (September 2014 and Typhoon Lando (October 18, 2015). The extent of damage or casualties affected only during typhoon Glenda where 835 persons were affected and 167 families were dislocated. At least fifty (50) houses were totally damaged and 100 houses were partially marred based on Disaster Report as of July 2014. Tornado had also hit Sitio Southridge 1, 2&

- 3 in Barangay San Antonio last May 6, 2015, whereas 65 families were affected and 56 houses were partially damaged. In Barangay UBL, flash flooding had hit the area last May 29, 2015 and 12 families were affected and 12 houses were partially damaged.


###### Storm Surge/Laguna Lake Level Rise.

Storm surge is an abnormal rise of water generated by a storm, over and above the predicted astronomical tide. It’s the change in the water level that is due to the presence of the storm. Since storm surge is a difference between water levels, it does not have a reference level. Storm tide is the water level rise during a storm due to the combination of storm surge and the astronomical tide. Since storm tide is the combination of surge and tide, it does require a reference level. A 15 ft. storm surge on top of a high tide that is 2 ft. above mean sea level produces a 17 ft. storm tide (http://www.nws.noaa.gov/om/hurricane/resources/surge_intro.pdf).

According to DENR-MGB, storm surge at the Laguna Lake is caused by heavy rainfall that likely to cause storm tide where water level rises. The Susceptibility level of the City to Storm Surge is quite low and the more affected area is the shoreline Laguna Lake.

Liquefaction. Soil liquefaction describes a phenomenon whereby a saturated or partially saturated soil substantially loses strength and stiffness in response to an applied stress, usually earthquake shaking or other sudden change in stress condition, causing it to behave like a liquid.

Susceptibility in liquefaction were mostly in shoreline Barangays, such as, Brgy. Cuyab, San Roque, Landayan, Sto. Niño and Nueva, respectively.

#### Climate Change Vulnerability: Threats, Potentials and Impacts

###### 1.2.10.1 Vulnerability and Adaptability of San Pedro City

The Climate Change and Cities Initiative began with the Vulnerability and Adaptation Assessment-an evaluation based on a “Learning-by-doing strategy” – enabling urban sectors to better understand and factor in climate-related risks in development planning. In order to provide a supplement basis for rationalized development decisions, the Vulnerability and Adaptation Assessment specifically sought to elucidate the following:

-  The level of climate risk that key cities are exposed to;
-  The factors that are inherent to urban elements and the system that predisposes them to risks;
-  How well these factors are currently being managed; and
-  Whether these efforts are sufficient to cushion future impacts.


This Vulnerability and Adaptation Assessment report contains a multi-sector view of the hazards and impacts of climate change in San Pedro City, and the perceived level of threat to the development potential of the city. Cross-referencing this threat with existing initiatives to avert adverse impacts of these hazards, this report also describes the adaptation and disaster risk-reduction measures that the city has been pursuing.

This report focuses on climate-related risks, namely: flooding, storm surges, typhoons, sea level rise, and drought. Geologic hazards like liquefaction, earthquakes and tsunamis identified in the workshops are treated as pre-existing conditions that further increase sensitivity or susceptibility to climate risks and maybe integrated further by the city‘s as they update their local plans. In the case of San Pedro City, this Vulnerability and Adaption Assessment focused on the following climate change impacts, which the stakeholders collectively perceived:

-  Flooding – An overflow of bodies of water leading to the temporary inundation of areas not normally submerged as a result of rainfall variability and typhoons;
-  Typhoons – Strong cyclones indicated by severe winds; and
-  Drought – An extreme increase in temperature.


The sectors assessed, on the other hand, referred to the traditional sectors that make up local development and land use plans, namely:

-  Social – Population and demographic characteristics and basic social services that include health, education, housing, and protective services;
-  Economic – Major economic systems, such: as agriculture, industry/manufacturing, service-based economic activities, and tourism, including the enabling services that facilitate the functions of these systems;
-  Infrastructure – Accessibility and utility support infrastructure, such as transport, water and power supply, telecommunications, waste management facilities, and other


- strategic infrastructure supporting all sector functions resulting from government mandates.;
-  Environment – The natural environment, including ecosystems, critical habitats, and protected areas. Environment also includes natural resource management practices and initiatives of the public sector; and
-  Land use – Land use refers to the spatial spread of sector activities and resources within a city’s territorial boundaries as a result of local and national policies. While the institutional sector is among the traditional sectors detailed in the Comprehensive Development Plan, this report considered this as a concern subsumed in the assessment of adaptive capacity where related public sector programs, actions, and policies were reviewed.


Owing to the nature of outputs, the VAA report are intended to serve as initial guidance for retrofitting policies and plans after further validation is made through other analytical tools and more accurate data, including maps. The extent of analysis only goes as far as the available information retrieved from participating agencies and those shared by the participants in the workshops

###### 1.2.10.2 Vulnerability Assessment Framework

The key components of United Nations Habitat’s Vulnerability and Adaptation Assessment are the evaluation of the city’s relative vulnerability to various climate-related hazards, focusing on the three determinants that show the specific hazards and systems affected. Exposure is defined as the degree of climate stress upon a particular unit of analysis (i.e. the city or parts thereof), and may be characterized as a long-term change in climate conditions, or changes in climate variability, including the magnitude and frequency of extreme events focusing on the city. These determinants are indicated by empirical data on regional climate variability.

The second component is sensitivity, which shows the level of impacts of climate- induced, natural, and human-made hazards affecting various systems in a city. It is the degree to which systems are affected and refers to characteristics inherent to the system, or those factors and conditions the predispose an element or the system to risks. In this report, these refer largely to the physical and socio-economic attributes of exposed elements like people, places and institutions.

The third component is adaptive capacity, demonstrating the city’s autonomous and planned actions to climate change. This determinant zeroes in on mostly planned measures that directly or indirectly mitigate or temper the impacts of climate change – which is an integral to long-term system adjustments or adaptation. This assessment examined the present measures and how these would likely fare with increased risks due to changes in exposure and/or sensitivity factors.

###### 1.2.10.3 Climate Change Related and Implication

La Niña, Like El Niño are both natural cyclical occurrences of abnormal weather condition. The former will bring heavy rains or prolonged rainy period causing floods and/or strong winds which is expected on July to September every year. The latter brought long dry season, the strongest since 1982-1983.

This season, mitigating measures were proposed to cushion the impact of such perennial occurrences.

The World Meteorological Organization (WMO) reported that El Niño may be followed by a cold weather event known as La Niña. This phenomenon would bring abnormal cold to the Eastern Equatorial Pacific forecasted to begin from July to September. La Niñ a natural consequence of the temperatures of the ocean waters starting from the tropical belt in the South Pacific of North Peru.

###### HOW WILL IT AFFECT OUR LIVES, PROPERTIES, CROPS, MARINE RESOURCES AND OUR ECONOMY AS A WHOLE?

La Niña is predicted to bring damages around late July – September, The rains will be late this year according to the weather forecast worldwide. It is expected to bring with it strong winds and prolonged rainy days which could result in massive flooding in many areas in the country.

When La Niña comes, siltation of irrigation canals throughout the country will be expected thick because of the erosion of the watersheds that now need to be reforested. That means that the flow in the irrigation system will be insufficient to meet the demand of farmers. Thus, there will be a reduction of food production particularly rice and other crops. Furthermore, borers and other hibernating insects like locusts, land animals, snakes, and rats would most likely move away from underground nests and prey on crops encroach to higher grounds. These insects and animals might be a good indicator of the coming rains and floods, the La Niña. Fishermen and farmers must be watchful on these indicators for a given amount of time will spell damages and recovery if the situation can be acted accordingly. There should have allocation of drinking water in the urban centers or areas directly affected by flooding. There are big possibilities that intestinal diseases will affect the populations and the H-fever carried by mosquitoes from stagnant waters will affect the populace once rain and flooding commences and worsen.

###### VULNERABILITY ASSESSMENT

The Vulnerability Assessment tools were treated separately in the method adopted under the previous studies. These tools were then assessed to verify their operability at the provincial level, given the planning requirements, practices and institutional structures involved in the formulation of the PDPFP. The results of the assessment conducted under the Training Module Preparation Project revealed that the VA tools earlier developed would pose operational difficulties in their mainstreaming to PDPFP due to several reasons.

Because mainstreaming climate change adaptation into local development planning is relatively new among local government units, were designed to facilitate the realization of the expected output and provide valuable results in the mainstreaming efforts. The set of indicators and scaling classes in the vulnerability tool for the five sectors lend themselves to continuous improvement as the wealth of experience and more robust data base are acquired by the provincial, municipal and city LGUs. Thus, the VA tool is considered a living instrument that can transform into a more sophisticated tool as its users become more adept in its use and as more accurate and reliable data become available. It is anticipated that in the future, the new generations of provincial planners will be using more sophisticated, more accurate and area-specific quantitative and computer aided VA tools.

Vulnerability assessment approach especially for the impact of erosion to the forestry sector. It will include: 1) a brief presentation of the GIS-based vulnerability assessment, 2) the stepby-step procedures in the preparation of sensitivity, exposure, and adaptive capacity subindicators, 3) creating composite indicator maps, and 4) combining the composite indicator maps to generate the sectoral impact vulnerability map.

Vulnerability indicator was assigned scale ratings and weights. Each vulnerability indicator (sensitivity, exposure and adaptive capacity) are composed of sub-indicators. To further illustrate, the sensitivity vulnerability indicator for erosion is composed of slope, rainfall, land cover, and soil sub- indicators. Sub-indicators (i.e. slope for the sensitivity vulnerability indicator) will have variations in scales (i.e. 0-8%, 8-18%, etc.) and are expressed in five scales, namely: Very high, High, Moderate, Low and Very Low with an assigned numerical value 1, 0.8, 0.6, 0.4, and 0.2, respectively. A measure of 1.0 is interpreted as having a high contribution to the vulnerability indicator while a score of 0.2 represents a low contribution to the vulnerability indicator.

Apart from the scale rating, each sub-indicator will have an assigned weight. Weight shall indicate the overall influence of the sub-indicator to the vulnerability indicator and the cumulative weights of the sub-indicators shall always total 1.0 (100%). The higher the assigned weight, the higher is the influence of the particular sub-indicator to the vulnerability indicator. Assigning scale ratings and weights shall be done in the attribute data table of each sub- indicator.

###### AREAS PRONE TO NATURAL HAZARDS

Moreover, all the municipalities along the lakeshore are prone to liquefaction. Garbage collection if inefficiently carried out in the Metro Manila and other urban or habitation centers can bring more diseases as this garbage will certainly return to people’s backyards because of floods.

Effects of La Niña are floods, continuous raining and diseases related to contain water due to flooding such as acute gastro arthritis, typhoid fever, cholera, Hepatitis A, dengue and Leptospirosis.

###### EL NIÑO:

This phenomenon can be described as a type of abnormal warming that occurs on the surface ocean waters in the part of the eastern tropical pacific that is known as the southern oscillation.

The effects of El Niño are temperature anomalies change in precipitation, variability, uncontrolled forest fire, fish kill and drought.

The El Niño phenomenon sometimes suffered in the province during summer, sometimes encounter forest fire in Mt. San Cristobal San Pablo City and fish kill in some part of Laguna Lake. In drought aspect no problem because of protected watershed, forested mountains and abundant water resources.

### Population and Settlements

![image 109](912157126-San-Pedro-Comprehensive-Development-Plan_images/imageFile109.png)

#### Key Issues and Concerns

- 1. Uneven High to Low Barangay Population Density driving urban congestion, pollution, crime incidence aggravation and high cost of social services delivery. There is also difficulty in the maintenance of peace and order in the City.
- 2. Increasing number of Informal Settlers due to uncontrolled urban migration and high population growth. Resulting to bigger demands for government services, improper waste disposal due to poor sanitation facilities. Clogged drainage canals and water ways are encountered because some of them raised their dwellings at the easement of estero, waters ways and canals.
- 3. Limited Land Area limiting expansion of residence, institutions and industry. Thereby lessening the potential capabilities of the local government to earn particularly on the business industry.
- 4. Irrational Land Utilization and Zoning Ordinance Compliance and Enforcement resulting to scattered and sporadic development.
- 5. Presence of idle lands, abandoned factory sites and warehouses and underutilized commercial buildings instead of making them productive as real property assets. These are prone to invasion of informal settlers and may also results to the possibility of venues for illegal activities.
- 6. Exploring the utilization of Laguna Lake for mixed use waterfront development. Reclamation of a portion of the lake to serve as the new site for business activities in the City.
- 7. Need to undertake urban renewal of the traditional urban core in the eastern side threatened by deterioration over the years.


###### 1.3.1.1 Sector Description

The largest land use of City of San Pedro is residential, which totals to one thousand one hundred seven (1,107.04) hectare or 48.98% of the total land area of 2,260 hectares. It shows that majority of the land use in San Pedro still focus on residential use, housing projects or low cost housing subdivisions are evidently present in all the 27 barangays. To address the increasing population significant changes are proposed, the shift towards intensification of residential land uses and promotion of mixed-uses with predominating residential uses.

There is trend of increasing population due to influx of migrants from other nearby cities and municipalities who have decided to live in the community. Most of the population only considers San Pedro as a dwelling place while most economic and employment opportunities are found outside the City. This is one of the reasons why San Pedro became a dormitory type of City for it serves as gateways to Cavite and Muntinlupa and providing easy access to Metro Manila.

At present, the City of San Pedro has experienced rapid sub-urbanization, as residential subdivisions were developed to cater to its growing workforce. This ongoing sub-urbanization has caused the splintering infrastructure and conflicts to its land uses. These are evident in the lack of buffer zones for pollutive land uses and danger zones, the water zones that need

to be revived since they have been neglected, its underutilized lake front despite being an amenity to the city and niches of mixed-uses found within residential areas. Residents and commercial establishments from the lowland experienced prolonged flooding.

The population increase will mean increase in the demand for government services, educational, law enforcement, medical and other public service needs. The City Government should allocate substantial budget to address these areas of concern.

#### Population Profile

###### 1.3.2.1 Population Size and Growth Trends

The city’s population generally increased from 1903 to 2020, though the annual growth rates exhibited a fluctuating but positive trend. In 1903, San Pedro logged a total population of 3,678 residents, increasing steadily with a growth rate of 0.82% until the year 1918. The population as well as the growth rates exhibited an increasing trend from then on until 1970 (8.68%). However, the growth rate dropped from 1970 to 1975 (5.68%), then showed a tremendous increase from 1975 to 1980 (11.40%). The growth rate between 1980 to 1990 and 1990 to 1995 declined gradually with growth rates of 7.70% and 3.63%, respectively but eventually picked up between 1995 to 2000 with the growth rate of 4.40%. Since year 2000, the growth rate has constantly declined reaching 0.01% growth rate by year 2020. It is relatively lower than the provincial and regional growth rate of 2.30% and 2.48%, respectively. Based on the 2020 Census of Population and Housing (CPH), San Pedro reached a total population of 326,001 as of 2020.

###### 1.3.2.2 Population Distribution and Density

The City of San Pedro obtained a total population of 319,519 based on the 2022 Localized Community-Based Monitoring System (CBMS), recording a growth rate of -0.8% from 2020 to 2022. The decline is due to COVID-19 pandemic, forcing migration to nearby cities and other provinces There is about 81,906 households with an average household size of 4 and gross population density of 13,280 people per square kilometer. Out of 27 barangays, 19 are considered urban, based on the Philippine Statistics Authority (PSA) classification of having a population size of 5,000 or more, constituting for 90.65% of the total population while the remaining 8 barangays are considered rural, accounting for 9.35% of the total population.

Brgy. San Antonio is the most populated barangay with a total of 58,706 (18.37%), while Brgy. Narra has the least population with a total of 2,251 (0.70%). Brgy. Estrella is the most densely populated barangay with 1,144 persons per hectare which is one of the urban barangays in the city.

###### 1.3.2.3 Population Composition

San Pedro is dominated by the female population against the male population where the majority are in the 20-24 years old age-group. Based on the 2022 Localized CBMS data, the female population is slightly higher than the male population, with a total number of 161,915 or 50.7% and 157,604 or 49.3% of the total population, respectively. Sixty-nine percent (69%) of the total household population comprised the working age group in the city, while children below 15 years of age comprised 24%, and the remaining 7% are aged 65 years old and above. Overall dependency ratio was 44, where for every 100 working –age people, 35 are young dependents aged 0-14 years old while 9 are the old dependents aged 65 years old and above.

Labor Force Participation Rate in the city was 80%, having males (81%) slightly higher than females (79%). San Pedro has a literacy rate of 98.5 as of 2022.

###### 1.3.2.4 Population Exposed to Flood Hazard

Barangays Cuyab, San Roque and Landayan are the three (3) barangays that are most susceptible to floods due to their location being near the Laguna lakeshore. Barangay Landayan, has a total population of 22,755 exposed to flood hazards, followed by Barangay Cuyab with a total population of 22,259 being exposed to flood hazards, and lastly, followed by Barangay San Roque having a total population of 5,017. Table 13 below shows the population exposure table to floods.

#### Patterns of Urban Development

Over the years, developments in San Pedro centered in the old traditional urban core located in the eastern side portion of Laguna Lake. There is danger and threat that the core areas will further stagnate and be left behind in terms of commercial development in view of the congestion in the highly dense inefficiently built up and flood prone areas near the Lake. Said areas should be master planned for redevelopment and renewal to revitalize its sustainable growth and development.

The remaining green undeveloped areas (see Figure 8) in the upland areas bordering Cavite Province should be master planned according to the UN Habitat model of Planned City Extension (PCE) so called “City within a City” concept. However, proposed land use map can be found in San Pedro CLUP.

In the future, Laguna Lake will become the new corridors of development for mixed used as contained in the Calabarzon Regional Development plan. The areas fronting San Pedro can be master planned waterfront development areas with the LLDA as the regulator in joint venture with the private sector under the PPP arrangement. It will kick start the ecological conservation of Laguna Lake and private business will come in providing thousands of employment to the formal and informal sector including the fishermen and MSMEs workers.

###### 1.3.3.1 Income and Poverty Incidence

Comparatively, San Pedro can be considered one of the least poor Cities around the country. Based on the data from NSCB, Laguna Province is one of the least poverty incidences among the families with a rate of 4.6 percent as of 2012. San Pedro residents can be classified into five groups according to their range of income as shown below.

Table 17. Five Types of Income Group

|INCOME GROUP|RANGE OF INCOME|TYPE OF WORK|
|---|---|---|
|1st|Below Php 5,000|Tricycle / pedicab drivers and household helpers|
|2nd|Php 5,000 – Php 10,000|Contractual employees of the government and private companies|
|3rd|Php 10,000 – Php 15,000|Regular employees from the private companies and government|
|4th|Php 15,000 – Php 20,000|Regular employees from the private companies and government|
|5th|Above Php 20,000|Regular employees from the private companies and government|

![image 115](912157126-San-Pedro-Comprehensive-Development-Plan_images/imageFile115.png)

Informal Settler Family Population

###### 1.3.4.1 Magnitude of the Informal Settler Families Population

The population of the City for 2022 is 319,519; about 3.% of the total population of the city is considered informal settlers or an estimate of 2,380 households. These informal settlers are scattered City wide, the biggest population of ISF about 5,733 are occupying private properties, these are properties that were not secured by the owners thereby making it accessible for ISF invasion.

There are ISFs living along the so-called “danger areas” such as creek/estero, seashore, railways, road lot and under the bridge this is about 1.46% of the population of the City. The remaining population of 1,699 or .5% of the ISF is situated at the different government properties such as open space and under NAPOCOR lines/properties.

About 323 are households that will be affected by government infra projects, 248 households with pending demolition threats situated along the shores of Laguna Lake. 174 households are displaced due to flooding and landslide vulnerability. About 2,838 are occupying private properties whose owners are willing to sell their property to the occupants under the CMP.

The unregulated influx and expansion of the informal sector families’ population in the future will be a danger and threat to the City. Development controls are needed through the strict implementation of zoning policies geared towards preventing urban sprawl and the proliferation of slum communities. The Barangay officials being the forefront of the government must be vigilant in safe guarding the open spaces in their respective areas. They must ensure that the existing ISF population in their barangays will remain dormant. They should introduce methods to regulate and control the growth of their population and stop further migration of new ISF in the City.

### The Local Economy

![image 117](912157126-San-Pedro-Comprehensive-Development-Plan_images/imageFile117.png)

#### Key Issues and Concerns

- 1. No so many big companies paying business and real property taxes and providing jobs.
- 2. Absence of High Salary Knowledge and Creativity Based Industry like IT BPO for PEZA accreditation.
- 3. Absence of High End Business Parks by big developers.
- 4. Limited Land Areas for secondary and tertiary sector expansion.
- 5. Low Performing for Upscaling Government Economic Enterprises like the Town Center, Abattoir, Public Market, Public Cemetery, among others.
- 6. Need to modernize the home based micro, small and medium enterprises particularly the sampaguita flower and aqua culture fishing industry.
- 7. Need to level up the religious tourism industry to international standards for TIEZA accreditation.
- 8. Need to master plan and develop Laguna Lake portions of San Pedro into mixed use for the expansion of secondary and tertiary sectors for PEZA accreditation.
- 9. Need to master plan and develop the large green areas in the upland for business park operations for possible PEZA accreditation.
- 10. There is growing number of eyesore, underutilized, idle and marginal properties that can be retrofitted for productive use and can be PEZA accredited. Economic Role of San Pedro


![image 118](912157126-San-Pedro-Comprehensive-Development-Plan_images/imageFile118.png)

San Pedro belongs to the economic hubs of Laguna situated in the 1st and the 2nd districts such as Biñan, Santa Rosa, Cabuyao and Calamba. These cities are highly industrialized areas playing a key role in Laguna and the Philippines economic development by contributing substantial foreign exchange earnings and providing employment and income to the local people. Eventually, San Pedro will become a Secondary Metropolitan Center by 2020 as envisioned in the Provincial Development and Physical Framework Plan of the Province of Laguna. It will fit well in the commercial – industrial – cum tourism development of San Pedro.

![image 119](912157126-San-Pedro-Comprehensive-Development-Plan_images/imageFile119.png)

#### Labor / Work Force and Income

As of 2022, the city has a literacy rate of 98.5% and an employment rate of 68%. Highly literate, English speaking, San Pedro residents are hard-working and very productive many of whom are working in the business establishments of Metro Manila. Eventually, there will be reverse migration of the working population from Metro Manila to the City of San Pedro once the commercial industrial development gathers momentum.

Total Family income and employment of the work force comes from the following business and industry: wholesale / retail trade, manufacturing, transport, banking and finance, real estate, electricity, gas and water, construction, hotels, education, health and working as overseas workers.

There is no income from large scale agricultural production except small fishing and commercial fish pens by big operators, and no forestry, mining and quarrying. There are no

industrial estates unlike in Biñan, Santa Rosa, Cabuyao and Calamba. However, PEZA can register and accredit buildings if productions and services are for export and outsourcing which can be an opportunity for San Pedro.

![image 120](912157126-San-Pedro-Comprehensive-Development-Plan_images/imageFile120.png)

#### Economic, Industrial, Commercial and Informal Sector Activities

The increasing business activity in San Pedro has made the City an emerging growth center in the Calabarzon Region in addition to its close proximity to Metro Manila supported by excellent road and transport facilities. However, the fast pace urbanization of the City has social and economic costs and consequences such as traffic congestion, air and water pollution, increasing demand and costs for delivery of basic services to the burgeoning population heightened by urban migration not only of highly productive work force but also the informal settlers.

The main economic activity in San Pedro is derived from the service sector, followed by the manufacturing industry and last by the rapidly vanishing agricultural sector such as the cultivation of sampaguita flower which needs revival, as well as the livestock and fishing industry.

There are no more rice producing agricultural areas in the lowland as well as in the upland Barangays. For this reason, it has to import rice from the other provinces through the rice traders.

Urban agriculture can be commercialized to meet the vegetable needs of the households should be promoted at the barangay level due to vegetable high prices and shortage of supply in certain periods of the year. As a highly urbanized city of the future, modern urban agriculture is needed such as vertical farming, aqua culture, hydroponic gardening, among others for reasons of food security.

No specific land allocated for the so called Strategic Agriculture and Fisheries Development Zone (SAFDZ) except the large scale fish pens operated commercially by businessmen for which San Pedro shares income from the LLDA fees and charges.

The low profile informal sector of the underground economy is flourishing providing jobs and livelihood to the low income population particularly the informal sector families. MSMEs products and services should be developed and promoted as they are labor intensive with downstream supply chain linkages to the big manufacturing industries in the neighboring economic zones operating in Biñan, Santa Rosa, Cabuyao and Calamba.

Micro, small and medium enterprises are not so visible especially the home based cottage industry in some barangays that needs shared technological modernization and capitalization infusion.

Commercial development had been increasing over the last decades such as retail stores, banks, insurance companies, restaurants, fast food, eateries, repair shops, automotive and motorcycle parts stores, transportation companies, hospital and medical clinics, gasoline stations, motels, schools and colleges, real estate companies, among others.

The currently established commercial establishments are the Madison Square, San Pedro Town Center, Pacita Commercial Center, Shopwise, Suki Market, Savemore, Puregold, Sogo, Pagcor Casino, Public Market, among others.

Commercial activity is very pronounced at the Poblacion Area as the Central Business District wherein demand for commercial services are brought about by the new City Hall, the old Municipal Building, Public Market, Suki Market, banking institutions and the Roman Catholic Church.

Pacita Complex is another commercial center driven by the huge residential areas that extends all the way and beyond the South Luzon Expressway (SLEX). Along the National Highway from Biñan to Muntinlupa are a mixed of numerous business establishments without proper zoning classifications by types of products and services.

The marginal business establishments are becoming to be a eyesore especially the abandoned buildings, warehouses, underutilized properties, among others. If retrofitted, they can become office buildings, warehouses, workers housing, supermarkets, and bank branches, among others.

Industrial establishments are located mostly in Barangay San Antonio like Alaska Milk Corporation, Universal Robina Corporation, Leslies Philippines, Drugmakers, FVC Philippines, among others. Establishments that closed down in the past include Aclem Paper, Cosmos, Kimberly Clark, others.

There is however high potential for new type of business voice and non-voice T-BPO related services, logistics and warehousing, high end business park and recreation in the upland areas connected to Bacoor and Dasmariñas in the southwest upland areas. A lone IT BPO Company – Filweb Systems Technology, operates in San Pedro. Robinson Property is poised to establish its own Mall along the National Highway most likely to be followed by SM Mall in a healthy competition to attract more businesses.

San Pedro local business community definitely has external linkages to international business community through exports and imports, banking and foreign exchange transactions, stocks and currency trading by local business. It contributes to the local domestic business development earning dollars, generating tax income and other fees and revenues for the government.
